\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}
is defined
by
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\alpha)=\alpha
and
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\kappa(\bar{\tau}))=\kappa(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{\tau}))
.
The term-to-term version is defined by

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)=\begin{cases}z_{\kern 0.2917ptt}&\text{if $t=x$ or $t$ is fluid}\\
{\mathsf{lam}}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\tau),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\mathcalx{B}_{x}(u)))&\text{if $t=(\lambda x\mathbin{:}\tau.\;u)$ and $t$ is not fluid}\\
{\mathsf{f}}_{k}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{\tau}),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{u}_{k}))&\text{if $t={\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{u}_{k}$}\end{cases}

For example, let

s=\lambda y.\>{\mathsf{f}}\>y\>(\lambda w.\>{\mathsf{g}}\>(y\>w))

where
y
has type
\kappa\rightarrow\kappa
and
w
has type
\kappa
.
We have

\mathcalx{B}_{y}({\mathsf{f}}\>y\>(\lambda w.\>{\mathsf{g}}\>(y\>w)))={\mathsf{f}}\>{\mathsf{db}}^{0}(\lambda w.\>{\mathsf{g}}\>({\mathsf{db}}^{1}\>w))

and

\mathcalx{B}_{w}({\mathsf{g}}\>({\mathsf{db}}^{1}\>w))={\mathsf{g}}\>({\mathsf{db}}^{1}\>{\mathsf{db}}^{0})
.
Neither
s
nor
\lambda w.\>{\mathsf{g}}\>(y\>w)
are fluid.
Hence,
we have

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)={\mathsf{lam}}({\rightarrow}(\kappa,\kappa),\allowbreak{\mathsf{f}}_{2}({\mathsf{db}}^{0}_{0},{\mathsf{lam}}(\kappa,{\mathsf{g}}_{1}({\mathsf{db}}^{1}_{1}({\mathsf{db}}^{0}_{0}))))
.

Definition 3.28
(Derived strict term order)

Let the strict term order derived from
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
be
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}
where
t\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}s
if
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)\succ_{\kern-0.5833pt{\mathsf{fo}}}\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)
.

We will show that the derived
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}
fulfills all properties
of a strict term order (Definition
3.5
)
if
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
fulfills the corresponding properties on first-order terms.
For the nonstrict term order
\succsim
, we can use the reflexive closure

\succeq_{{\lambda}}
of
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}
.

Lemma 3.29

Let
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
be a strict partial order on first-order terms and
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}

the derived term order on
\beta\eta
-equivalence classes. If the restriction of

\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
to ground terms enjoys well-foundedness, totality, the subterm
property, and compatibility with contexts

(
w.r.t.
first-order terms
)
,
the restriction of
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}
to
ground terms enjoys
well-foundedness, totality, the green subterm
property, and compatibility with green contexts

(
w.r.t.

\beta\eta
-equivalence classes
)
.

Proof

Transitivity and irreflexivity of
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}

imply transitivity and irreflexivity of
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}
.

Well-foundedness
: If there existed an infinite chain
t_{1}\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}t_{2}\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}\cdots
of ground terms, there would also be the
chain
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t_{1})\succ_{\kern-0.5833pt{\mathsf{fo}}}\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t_{2})\succ_{\kern-0.5833pt{\mathsf{fo}}}\cdots
,
contradicting the well-foundedness of
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
on ground
\lambda
-free terms.

Totality:
By ground totality of
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
, for any ground terms
t
and
s
we have

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)\succ_{\kern-0.5833pt{\mathsf{fo}}}\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)
,

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)\prec_{\mathsf{fo}}\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)
,
or
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)
.
In the first two cases, it follows that
t\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}s
or
t\prec_{\lambda}s
.
In the last case, it follows that
t=s

because
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}
is clearly injective.

Green subterm property:
Let
s
be a term. We show that
s\succeq_{{\lambda}}s|_{p}
by induction on
p
, where

s|_{p}
denotes the green subterm at position
p
. If
p=\varepsilon
, this is trivial.
If
p=p^{\prime}.i
, we have
s\succeq_{{\lambda}}s|_{p^{\prime}}
by the induction hypothesis.
Hence, it suffices to show that
s|_{p^{\prime}}\succeq_{{\lambda}}s|_{p^{\prime}.i}
.
From the existence of the position
p^{\prime}.i
,
we know that
s|_{p^{\prime}}
must be of the form

s|_{p^{\prime}}={\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{u}_{k}
.
Then
s|_{p^{\prime}.i}=u_{i}
.
The encoding yields

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s|_{p^{\prime}})={\mathsf{f}}_{k}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{\tau}),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{u}_{k}))

and hence
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s|_{p^{\prime}})\succeq_{\kern-0.2917pt{\mathsf{fo}}}\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s|_{p^{\prime}.i})

by the ground subterm property of
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
. Hence,

s|_{p^{\prime}}\succeq_{{\lambda}}s|_{p^{\prime}.i}
and thus
s\succeq_{{\lambda}}s|_{p}
.

Compatibility with green contexts:
By induction on the depth of the context,
it suffices to show that
t\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}s
implies

{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{u}\>t\>\bar{v}\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{u}\>s\>\bar{v}

for all
t
,
s
,
{\mathsf{f}}
,
\bar{\tau}
,
\bar{u}
, and
\bar{v}
.
This amounts to showing that

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)\succ_{\kern-0.5833pt{\mathsf{fo}}}\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)
implies

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}({\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{u}\>t\>\bar{v})={\mathsf{f}}_{k}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{\tau}),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{u}),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{v}))\succ_{\kern-0.5833pt{\mathsf{fo}}}{\mathsf{f}}_{k}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{\tau}),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{u}),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{v}))=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}({\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{u}\>s\>\bar{v})
,
which follows directly from ground compatibility of
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
with
contexts and the induction hypothesis.
∎

Lemma 3.30

Let
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
be a strict partial order on first-order terms. If
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}

is stable under grounding substitutions
(
w.r.t.
first-order
terms
)
, the derived term order
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}
is
stable under grounding substitutions
(
w.r.t.

\beta\eta
-equivalence classes
)
.

Proof

Assume
s\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}s^{\prime}
for some terms
s
and
s^{\prime}
.
Let
\theta
be a higher-order substitution grounding
s
and
s^{\prime}
.
We must show
s\theta\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}s^{\prime}\theta
.
We will define a first-order substitution
\rho

grounding
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)
and
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s^{\prime})

such that
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)\rho=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s\theta)
and
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s^{\prime})\rho=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s^{\prime}\theta)
.
Since
s\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}s^{\prime}
, we have
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)\succ_{\kern-0.5833pt{\mathsf{fo}}}\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s^{\prime})
.
By stability of
\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\mathsf{fo}}}
under grounding substitutions,

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)\rho\succ_{\kern-0.5833pt{\mathsf{fo}}}\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s^{\prime})\rho
.
It follows that

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s\theta)\succ_{\kern-0.5833pt{\mathsf{fo}}}\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s^{\prime}\theta)

and hence
s\theta\succ_{\kern-0.68283pt{\lambda}}s^{\prime}\theta
.

We define the first-order substitution
\rho
as
\alpha\rho=\alpha\theta
for
type variables
\alpha
and
z_{\kern 0.34148ptu}\rho=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(u\theta)
for terms
u
.
Strictly speaking, the domain of a substitution must be finite, so we restrict
this definition of
\rho
to the finitely many variables that occur
in the computation of
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)
and
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s^{\prime})
.

Clearly
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\tau)\rho=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\tau\theta)
for all types
\tau
occurring
in the computation of
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)
and
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s^{\prime})
.
Moreover,
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)\rho=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t\theta)
for all
t
occurring
in the computation of
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s)
and
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(s^{\prime})
, which we show by induction on the
definition of the encoding.
If
t=x
or if
t
is fluid,
\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)\rho=z_{\kern 0.2917ptt}\rho=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t\theta)
.
If
t={\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{u}
,
then

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)\rho={\mathsf{f}}_{k}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{\tau})\rho,\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{u})\rho)\overset{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\text{IH}}}{=}{\mathsf{f}}_{k}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{\tau}\theta),\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\bar{u}\theta))=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}({\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\theta\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>(\bar{u}\theta))=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t\theta)
.
If
t=(\lambda x\mathbin{:}\tau.\;u)
and
t
is not fluid, then

\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t)\rho={\mathsf{lam}}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\tau)\rho,\allowbreak\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\mathcalx{B}_{x}(u))\rho)\overset{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\text{IH}}}{=}{\mathsf{lam}}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\tau\theta),\allowbreak\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\mathcalx{B}_{x}(u)\theta))={\mathsf{lam}}(\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\tau\theta),\allowbreak\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\mathcalx{B}_{x}(u)\theta[x\mapsto x]))=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(\lambda x\mathbin{:}\tau\theta.\;u\theta[x\mapsto x])=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}((\lambda x\mathbin{:}\tau.\;u)\theta)=\mathcalx{\kern-0.41672ptO}(t\theta)
. ∎

4
Refutational Completeness

Besides soundness, the most important property of the Boolean-free

\lambda
-superposition calculus introduced in Sect.
3

is refutational completeness.
We will prove static and dynamic refutational completeness of
\mathit{HInf}

w.r.t.

(\mathit{HRed}_{\mathrm{I}},{\mathit{HRed}}_{\mathrm{C}})
, which is defined as follows:

Definition 4.1
(Static refutational completeness)

Let
\mathit{Inf}
be an inference system and let
(\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{I}},\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{C}})
be a redundancy criterion.
The inference system
\mathit{Inf}
is
statically refutationally complete

w.r.t.

(\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{I}},\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{C}})
if
we have
N\models\bot
if and only if
\bot\in N

for every clause set
N
that is saturated
w.r.t.

\mathit{Inf}
and
\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{I}}
.

Definition 4.2
(Dynamic refutational completeness)

Let
\mathit{Inf}
be an inference system and let
(\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{I}},\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{C}})
be a redundancy criterion.
Let
(N_{i})_{i}
be a finite or infinite sequence over sets of clauses.
Such a sequence is
a
derivation
if

N_{i}\setminus N_{i+1}\subseteq\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{C}}(N_{i+1})
for all
i
.
It is
fair
if all
\mathit{Inf}
-inferences from clauses in
the limit inferior
\bigcup_{i}\bigcap_{\!j\geq i}N_{\!j}
are contained in

\bigcup_{i}\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{I}}(N_{i})
.
The inference system
\mathit{Inf}
is
dynamically refutationally complete

w.r.t.

(\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{I}},\mathit{Red}_{\mathrm{C}})
if
for every fair derivation
(N_{i})_{i}
such that
N_{0}\models\bot
,
we have
\bot\in N_{i}
for some
i
.

4.1

Outline of the Proof

The proof proceeds in three steps, corresponding to the three levels
{\mathrm{GF}}
,
{\mathrm{GH}}
, and
{\mathrm{H}}
introduced in Sect.
3.4
:

1.

We use Bachmair and Ganzinger’s work on the refutational completeness of standard
(first-order) superposition
bachmair-ganzinger-1994

to prove static refutational completeness of
\mathit{GFInf}
.

2.

From the first-order model constructed in Bachmair and Ganzinger’s proof,
we derive a clausal higher-order model and thus prove static refutational completeness of
\mathit{GHInf}
.

3.

We use the saturation framework by Waldmann et al.
waldmann-et-al-2020-saturation
to lift the static refutational completeness of
\mathit{GHInf}

to static and dynamic refutational completeness of
\mathit{HInf}
.

In the first step,
since the inference system
\mathit{GFInf}
is standard ground superposition,
we can make use of Bachmair and Ganzinger’s
results. Given a saturated clause set
N\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GF}}
with
\bot\not\in N
,
Bachmair and Ganzinger prove refutational completeness by constructing
a term rewriting system
R_{N}
and showing that it can be
viewed as an interpretation that is a model of
N
.
This first step deals exclusively with ground first-order clauses.

In the second step, we derive refutational completeness of
\mathit{GHInf}
.
Given a saturated clause set
N\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
with
\bot\not\in N
,
we use the first-order model
R_{\mathcalx{F}\!(N)}
of
\mathcalx{F}\!(N)
constructed in the first step
to derive a clausal higher-order interpretation that is a model of
N
.
Under the encoding
\mathcalx{F}
,
occurrences of the same symbol with different numbers of arguments are
regarded as different symbols—e.g.,

\mathcalx{F}\!({\mathsf{f}})={\mathsf{f}}_{0}

and

\mathcalx{F}\!({\mathsf{f}}\>{\mathsf{a}})={\mathsf{f}}_{1}({\mathsf{a}}_{0})
.
All
\lambda
-expressions
\lambda x.\>t
are regarded as uninterpreted
symbols
{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>t}
.
The difficulty is to
construct a higher-order interpretation
that merges the first-order denotations of all
{\mathsf{f}}_{i}
into a single
higher-order denotation of
{\mathsf{f}}

and to show that the symbols
{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>t}
behave like

\lambda x.\>t
. This step relies on saturation

w.r.t.
the
GArgCong
rule—which connects a
term of functional type with its value when applied to an argument
x
—and on
the presence of the extensionality rule
GExt
.

In the third step,
we employ the saturation framework by Waldmann et al.
waldmann-et-al-2020-saturation
,
which is based on Bachmair and Ganzinger’s framework
(
bachmair-ganzinger-2001-resolution
, Sect. 4)
,
to prove refutational completeness of
\mathit{HInf}
.
Both saturation frameworks help calculus designers prove static and dynamic refutational completeness of nonground calculi.
In addition, the framework by Waldmann et al. explicitly supports the redundancy criterion defined in Sect.
3.4
,
which can be used to justify the deletion of subsumed clauses.
Moreover, their saturation framework provides completeness theorems for prover architectures, such as the DISCOUNT loop.

The main proof obligation we must discharge to use the framework is that there
should exist nonground inferences in
\mathit{HInf}
corresponding to all nonredundant inferences in
\mathit{GHInf}
.
We face two specifically higher-order difficulties.
First, in standard superposition, we can avoid
Sup
inferences into
variables
x
by exploiting the clause order’s compatibility with contexts:
If
t^{\prime}\prec t
, we have
C\{x\mapsto\penalty t^{\prime}\}\prec C\{x\mapsto t\}
,
which allows us to show that
Sup
inferences into
variables are redundant. This technique fails for higher-order variables
x

that occur applied in
C
, because the order lacks compatibility
with arguments. This is why our
Sup
rule must perform some inferences
into variables.
The other difficulty also concerns applied variables. We must show that any
nonredundant
Sup
inference in level
{\mathrm{GH}}
into a position corresponding
to a fluid term or a deeply occurring variable in level
{\mathrm{H}}
can be
lifted to a
FluidSup
inference. This involves showing that the
z

variable in
FluidSup
can represent arbitrary contexts around a
term
t
.

For the entire proof of refutational completeness,
\beta\eta
-normalization is the
proverbial dog that did not bark. On level
{\mathrm{GH}}
, the rules

Sup
,
ERes
, and
EFact
preserve

\eta
-short
\beta
-normal form, and so does first-order term rewriting.
Thus, we can completely ignore
\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}_{\beta}
and

\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}_{\eta}
.
On level
{\mathrm{H}}
, instantiation can cause
\beta
- and
\eta
-reduction, but
this poses no difficulties thanks to the clause order’s stability
under grounding substitutions.

4.2

The Ground First-Order Level

We use Bachmair and Ganzinger’s results on standard superposition
bachmair-ganzinger-1994

to prove refutational completeness of
{\mathrm{GF}}
. In the subsequent steps, we will also make use of specific properties
of the model Bachmair and Ganzinger construct.
The basis of Bachmair and Ganzinger’s proof is that
a term rewriting system
R
defines an interpretation
\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GF}}/R

such that for every ground equation
s\approx t
, we have
\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GF}}/R\models s\approx t
if and only if
s\mathrel{\leftarrow\kern-7.25482pt\rightarrow}_{R}^{*}t
.
Formally,
\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GF}}/R
denotes the
monomorphic first-order interpretation
whose universes
\mathscr{U}_{\tau}
consist of the
R
-equivalence
classes over
\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GF}}
containing terms of type
\tau
.
The interpretation
\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GF}}/R
is term-generated—that is,
for every element
a
of the universe of this interpretation and for any
valuation
\xi
, there exists
a ground term
t
such that
\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GF}}/R}^{\xi}}=a
.
To lighten notation, we will write
R
to refer to both the term rewriting
system
R
and the interpretation
\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GF}}/R
.

The term rewriting system is constructed as follows:

Definition 4.3

Let
N\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GF}}
.
We first define sets of rewrite rules
E_{N}^{C}
and
R_{N}^{C}
for all
C\in N
by induction on the clause order.
Assume that
E_{N}^{D}
has already been defined for all
D\in N

such that
D\prec C.
Then
R_{N}^{C}=\bigcup_{D\prec C}E_{N}^{D}.

Let
E_{N}^{C}=\{s\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}t\}
if the following conditions are met:

(a)

C=C^{\prime}\lor s\approx t
;

(b)

s\approx t
is
\succsim
-maximal in
C
;

(c)

s\succ t
;

(d)

C^{\prime}
is false in
R_{N}^{C}
;

(e)

s
is irreducible
w.r.t.

R_{N}^{C}.

Then
C
is said to
produce

s\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}t
. Otherwise,
E_{N}^{C}=\emptyset
.
Finally,
R_{N}=\bigcup_{D}E_{N}^{D}.

Based on Bachmair and Ganzinger’s work,
Bentkamp et al.
(
bentkamp-et-al-lfhosup-arxiv
, Lemma 4.2 and Theorem 4.3)
prove the following properties of
R_{N}
:

Lemma 4.4

Let
\bot\not\in N
and
N\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GF}}
be saturated
w.r.t.

\mathit{GFInf}

and
\mathit{GFRed}_{\mathrm{I}}
. If
C=C^{\prime}\lor s\approx t\in N
produces
s\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}t
,
then
s\approx t
is strictly
\succeq
-eligible in
C
and
C^{\prime}
is false in
R_{N}
.

Theorem 4.5
(Ground first-order static refutational completeness)

The inference system
\mathit{GFInf}
is statically refutationally complete
w.r.t.

(\mathit{GFRed}_{\mathrm{I}},\mathit{GFRed}_{\mathrm{C}})
.
More precisely, if
N\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GF}}
is a clause set saturated
w.r.t.

\mathit{GFInf}

and
\mathit{GFRed}_{\mathrm{I}}
such that
\bot\not\in N
,
then
R_{N}
is a model of
N
.

4.3

The Ground Higher-Order Level

In this subsection, let
\mathit{GHSel}
be a selection function on
\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
,
let
N\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
be a clause set saturated
w.r.t.

\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
and
\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
such that
\bot\not\in N
. Clearly,
\mathcalx{F}\!(N)
is then saturated
w.r.t.

\smash{\mathit{GFInf}^{\mathcalx{F}\!(\mathit{GHSel})}}
and
\smash{\mathit{GFRed}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathcalx{F}\!(\mathit{GHSel})}}
.

We abbreviate
R_{\mathcalx{F}\!(N)}
as
R
. Given two terms
s,t\in\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GH}}
, we write
s\sim t
to abbreviate

R\models\mathcalx{F}\!(s)\approx\mathcalx{F}\!(t)
,
which is equivalent to

\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s)\rrbracket_{R}}=\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\rrbracket_{R}}
.

Lemma 4.6

For all terms
t,s:\tau\rightarrow\upsilon
in
\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GH}}
, the following statements are equivalent:

1.

t\sim s
;

2.

t\>({\mathsf{diff}}\>t\>s)\sim s\>({\mathsf{diff}}\>t\>s)
;

3.

t\>u\sim s\>u
for all
u\in\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GH}}
.

Proof

(3)
\Rightarrow
(2): Take
u:={\mathsf{diff}}\>t\>s
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1):
Since
N
is saturated, the
GExt
inference that generates
the clause
C=t\>({\mathsf{diff}}\>t\>s)\not\approx s\>({\mathsf{diff}}\>t\>s)\mathrel{\lor}t\approx s
is
redundant—i.e.,
C\in N\mathrel{\cup}\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}}(N)
—and hence

R\models\mathcalx{F}\!(C)
by
Theorem
4.5

and
the assumption that
\bot\not\in N
.
Therefore, it follows from

t\>({\mathsf{diff}}\>t\>s)\sim s\>({\mathsf{diff}}\>t\>s)
that
t\sim s
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(3):
We assume that
t\sim s
—i.e.,
\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\mathrel{\leftarrow\kern-7.25482pt\rightarrow}^{*}_{R}\mathcalx{F}\!(s)
.
By induction on the number of rewrite steps between
\mathcalx{F}\!(t)
and
\mathcalx{F}\!(s)

and by transitivity of
\sim
,
it suffices to show
that
\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}_{R}\mathcalx{F}\!(s)
implies
t\>u\sim s\>u
.
If the rewrite step
\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}_{R}\mathcalx{F}\!(s)
is not at the
top level, then neither
s\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}
nor
t\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}
can be
\lambda
-expressions.
Therefore,
(s\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\>(u\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})
and
(t\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\>(u\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})
are in

\eta
-short
\beta
-normal form,
and there is an analogous rewrite step
\mathcalx{F}\!(t\>u)\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}_{R}\mathcalx{F}\!(s\>u)

using the same rewrite rule.
It follows that
t\>u\sim s\>u
.
If the rewrite step
\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}_{R}\mathcalx{F}\!(s)
is at the
top level,

\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathcalx{F}\!(s)
must be a rule of
R
.
This rule must originate from a productive clause of the form

\mathcalx{F}\!(C)=\mathcalx{F}\!(C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}t\approx s)
.
By Lemma
4.4
,

\mathcalx{F}\!(t\approx s)
is
strictly
\succeq
-eligible in
\mathcalx{F}\!(C)

w.r.t.

\mathcalx{F}\!(\mathit{GHSel})
,
and hence
t\approx s
is
strictly
\succeq
-eligible in
C

w.r.t.

\mathit{GHSel}
.
Thus, the following

GArgCong
inference
\iota
is applicable:

\displaystyle{C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}t\approx s}\mathstrut

GArgCong

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}t\>u\approx s\>u\mathstrut

By saturation,

\iota
is redundant
w.r.t.

N
—i.e.,
\mathit{concl}(\iota)\in N\mathrel{\cup}\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}}(N)
.
By
Theorem
4.5

and the assumption
that
\bot\not\in N
,

\mathcalx{F}\!(\mathit{concl}(\iota))
is then true in
R
.
By Lemma
4.4
,

\mathcalx{F}\!(C^{\prime})
is false in
R
. Therefore,

\mathcalx{F}\!(t\>u\approx s\>u)
must be true in
R
. ∎

Lemma 4.7

Let
s\in\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{H}}
and
\theta
,
\theta^{\prime}
grounding
substitutions such that
x\theta\sim x\theta^{\prime}
for all variables
x

and
\alpha\theta=\alpha\theta^{\prime}
for all type variables
\alpha
.
Then
s\theta\sim s\theta^{\prime}
.

Proof

In this proof, we work directly on
\lambda
-terms. To prove the
lemma, it suffices to prove it
for any
\lambda
-term
s
.
Here, for
\lambda
-terms
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, the notation

t_{1}\sim t_{2}
is to be read as
t_{1}\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}\sim{t_{2}}\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}

because
\mathcalx{F}
is only defined on
\eta
-short
\beta
-normal terms.

Definition
We extend the syntax of
\lambda
-terms with a new
polymorphic function symbol
\oplus:\mathsf{\Pi}\alpha.\;\alpha\rightarrow\alpha\rightarrow\alpha
.
We will omit its type argument. It is equipped with two reduction rules:

\oplus\>t\>s\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}t
and
\oplus\>t\>s\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}s
.
A
\beta\oplus
-reduction step

is either a rewrite step following one of these rules or a
\beta
-reduction step.

The computability path order
\succ_{\mathsf{CPO}}

blanqui-et-al-2015
guarantees that

–

\oplus\>t\>s\succ_{\mathsf{CPO}}s
by applying rule
@\rhd
;

–

\oplus\>t\>s\succ_{\mathsf{CPO}}t
by applying rule
@\rhd
twice;

–

(\lambda x.\>t)\>s\succ_{\mathsf{CPO}}t[x\mapsto s]
by applying rule
@\beta
.

Since this order is moreover monotone, it decreases with
\beta\oplus
-reduction steps.
The order is also well founded; thus,
\beta\oplus
-reductions terminate.
And since the
\beta\oplus
-reduction steps describe a finitely branching term rewriting
system, by Kőnig’s lemma
koenigs-lemma-1927
, there is a maximal number of

\beta\oplus
-reduction steps
from each
\lambda
-term.

Definition
A
\lambda
-term is
term-ground
if it does not contain free term variables.
It may contain polymorphic type arguments.

Definition
We introduce an auxiliary function
\mathscr{S}

that essentially measures the size of a
\lambda
-term but assigns a size of
1
to
term-ground
\lambda
-terms.

\mathscr{S}(s)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $s$ is term-ground or is a bound or free variable or a symbol}\\
1+\mathscr{S}(t)&\text{if $s$ is not term-ground and has the form $\lambda x.\>t$}\\
\mathscr{S}(t)+\mathscr{S}(u)&\text{if $s$ is not term-ground and has the form $t\>u$}\end{cases}

We prove
s\theta\sim s\theta^{\prime}
by well-founded
induction on
s
,
\theta
, and
\theta^{\prime}
using
the left-to-right lexicographic order on the triple

\bigl(n_{1}(s),n_{2}(s),n_{3}(s)\bigr)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
, where

–

n_{1}(s)
is the maximal number of

\beta\oplus
-reduction steps starting from
s\sigma
, where

\sigma
is the substitution mapping each term variable
x

to
\oplus\>x\theta\>x\theta^{\prime}
;

–

n_{2}(s)
is the number of free term variables occurring more than once in
s
;

–

n_{3}(s)=\mathscr{S}(s)
.

Case 1:
The
\lambda
-term
s
is term-ground. Then the lemma is trivial.

Case 2:
The
\lambda
-term
s
contains
k\geq 2
free term variables. Then we can apply the induction
hypothesis twice and use the transitivity of
\sim
as follows. Let
x

be one of the free term variables in
s
. Let
\rho=\{x\mapsto x\theta\}
the
substitution that maps
x
to
x\theta
and ignores all other variables. Let

\rho^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}[x\mapsto x]
.

We want to invoke the induction hypothesis on
s\rho
and
s\rho^{\prime}
. This is
justified because
s\sigma

\oplus
-reduces to
s\rho\sigma
and to

s\rho^{\prime}\sigma
.
These
\oplus
-reductions have at least one step because
x
occurs in
s
and
k\geq 2
.
Hence,
n_{1}(s)>n_{1}(s\rho)
and
n_{1}(s)>n_{1}(s\rho^{\prime})
.

This application of the induction hypothesis gives us

s\rho\theta\sim s\rho\theta^{\prime}
and
s\rho^{\prime}\theta\sim s\rho^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}
.
Since
s\rho\theta=s\theta
and
s\rho^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}=s\theta^{\prime}
,
this is equivalent to

s\theta\sim s\rho\theta^{\prime}
and
s\rho^{\prime}\theta\sim s\theta^{\prime}
.
Since moreover
s\rho\theta^{\prime}=s\rho^{\prime}\theta
, we have
s\theta\sim s\theta^{\prime}
by
transitivity of
\sim
.
The following illustration visualizes the above argument:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\rho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\rho^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\theta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{IH}}{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\rho\theta^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\rho^{\prime}\theta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{IH}}{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\theta^{\prime}}
\theta

\theta^{\prime}
\theta

\theta^{\prime}

Case 3:
The
\lambda
-term
s
contains a free term variable that occurs more than once. Then we rename
variable occurrences apart by replacing each occurrence of each free term variable
x
by
a fresh variable
x_{i}
, for which we define
x_{i}\theta=x\theta
and

x_{i}\theta^{\prime}=x\theta^{\prime}
. Let
s^{\prime}
be the resulting
\lambda
-term. Since
s\sigma=s^{\prime}\sigma
, we have
n_{1}(s)=n_{1}(s^{\prime})
. All free term variables occur only once in
s^{\prime}
.
Hence,
n_{2}(s)>0=n_{2}(s^{\prime})
. Therefore, we can invoke the induction
hypothesis on
s^{\prime}
to obtain
s^{\prime}\theta\sim s^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}
. Since
s\theta=s^{\prime}\theta
and
s\theta^{\prime}=s^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}
, it follows that

s\theta\sim s\theta^{\prime}
.

Case 4:
The
\lambda
-term
s
contains only one free term variable
x
, which occurs exactly once.

Case 4.1:
The
\lambda
-term
s
is of the form
{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{t}

for some symbol
{\mathsf{f}}
, some types
\bar{\tau}
, and some

\lambda
-terms
\bar{t}
. Then let
u
be the
\lambda
-term in

\bar{t}
that contains
x
.
We want to apply the induction hypothesis to
u
, which can be
justified as follows. Consider the longest sequence of

\beta\oplus
-reductions from
u\sigma
. This sequence can be
replicated inside
s\sigma=({\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{t})\sigma
.
Therefore,
the longest sequence of
\beta\oplus
-reductions from
s\sigma
is at
least as long—i.e.,
n_{1}(s)\geq n_{1}(u)
. Since both
s
and
u
have
only one free term variable occurrence, we have
n_{2}(s)=0=n_{2}(u)
. But
n_{3}(s)>n_{3}(u)
because
u
is a term-nonground subterm of

s
.

Applying the induction hypothesis gives us
u\theta\sim u\theta^{\prime}
. By
definition of
\mathcalx{F}
, we have

\mathcalx{F}\!(({\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{t})\theta)={\mathsf{f}}_{m}^{\smash{\bar{\tau}\theta}}\>\mathcalx{F}\!(\bar{t}\theta)

and analogously for
\theta^{\prime}
,
where
m
is the length of
\bar{t}
.
By congruence of
\approx
in first-order logic, it follows that
s\theta\sim s\theta^{\prime}
.

Case 4.2:
The
\lambda
-term
s
is of the form
x\>\bar{t}
for some
\lambda
-terms
\bar{t}
. Then we
observe that, by assumption,
x\theta\sim x\theta^{\prime}
. By applying
Lemma
4.6
repeatedly, we have

x\theta\>\bar{t}\sim x\theta^{\prime}\>\bar{t}
. Since
x
occurs only once,

\bar{t}
is term-ground and hence
s\theta=x\theta\>\bar{t}
and
s\theta^{\prime}=x\theta^{\prime}\>\bar{t}
. Therefore,
s\theta\sim s\theta^{\prime}
.

Case 4.3:
The
\lambda
-term
s
is of the form
\lambda z.\>u
for some
\lambda
-term
u
. Then we observe that to prove

s\theta\sim s\theta^{\prime}
, it suffices to show that

s\theta\>({\mathsf{diff}}\>s\theta\>s\theta^{\prime})\sim s\theta^{\prime}\>({\mathsf{diff}}\>s\theta\>s\theta^{\prime})

by Lemma
4.6
. Via
\beta\eta
-conversion, this is equivalent to

u\rho\theta\sim u\rho\theta^{\prime}
where
\rho=\{z\mapsto{\mathsf{diff}}\>(s\theta\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\>(s\theta^{\prime}\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\}
.
To prove
u\rho\theta\sim u\rho\theta^{\prime}
, we apply the
induction hypothesis on
u\rho
.

It remains to show that the induction hypothesis is applicable on
u\rho
.
Consider the longest sequence of
\beta\oplus
-reductions from

u\rho\sigma
. Since
z\rho
starts with the
{\mathsf{diff}}
symbol,
z\rho

will not cause more
\beta\oplus
-reductions than
z
. Hence, the same
sequence of
\beta\oplus
-reductions can be applied inside
s\sigma=(\lambda z.\>u)\sigma
, proving that
n_{1}(s)\geq n_{1}(u\rho)
.
Since both
s
and
u\rho
have only one free term variable occurrence,

n_{2}(s)=0=n_{2}(u\rho)
. But
n_{3}(s)=\mathscr{S}(s)=1+\mathscr{S}(u)

because
s
is term-nonground. Moreover,

\mathscr{S}(u)\geq\mathscr{S}(u\rho)=n_{3}(u\rho)
because
\rho
replaces a
variable by a ground
\lambda
-term. Hence,
n_{3}(s)>n_{3}(u\rho)
, which justifies the application of the induction hypothesis.

Case 4.4:
The
\lambda
-term
s
is of the form
(\lambda z.\>u)\>t_{0}\>\bar{t}
for some
\lambda
-terms
u
,

t_{0}
, and
\bar{t}
.
We apply the induction hypothesis on
s^{\prime}=u\{z\mapsto t_{0}\}\>\bar{t}
.
To justify it, consider the longest sequence of
\beta\oplus
-reductions from

s^{\prime}\sigma
. Prepending the reduction
s\sigma\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}_{\beta}s^{\prime}\sigma
to it gives us a longer sequence from
s\sigma
. Hence,

n_{1}(s)>n_{1}(s^{\prime})
.
The induction hypothesis gives us
s^{\prime}\theta\sim s^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}
. Since

\sim
is invariant under
\beta
-reductions, it follows that

s\theta\sim s\theta^{\prime}
. ∎

We proceed by defining a higher-order interpretation

\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}=(\mathscr{U}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\mathscr{J}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\mathscr{J}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\allowbreak\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}})
derived from
R
.
The interpretation
R
is
an interpretation in monomorphic first-order logic.
Let
\mathscr{U}_{\tau}
be its universe for type
\tau

and
\mathscr{J}
its interpretation function.

To illustrate the construction, we will employ the following running example.
Let the higher-order signature be
\mathrm{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{ty}}=\{\iota,\rightarrow\}
and
\mathrm{\Sigma}=\{{\mathsf{f}}:\iota\rightarrow\iota,\>{\mathsf{a}}:\iota,\>{\mathsf{b}}:\iota\}
.
The first-order signature accordingly consists of
\mathrm{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{ty}}
and

\mathrm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{GF}}=\{{\mathsf{f}}_{0},{\mathsf{f}}_{1},{\mathsf{a}}_{0},{\mathsf{b}}_{0}\}\cup\{{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>t}\mid\lambda x.\>t\in\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GH}}\}
.
We write
[t]
for the equivalence class of
t\in\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GF}}
modulo
R
.
We assume that
[{\mathsf{f}}_{0}]=[{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>x}]
,

[{\mathsf{a}}_{0}]=[{\mathsf{f}}_{1}({\mathsf{a}}_{0})]
,

[{\mathsf{b}}_{0}]=[{\mathsf{f}}_{1}({\mathsf{b}}_{0})]
,
and that
{\mathsf{f}}_{0}
,
{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>{\mathsf{a}}}
,

{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>{\mathsf{b}}_{0}}
,
{\mathsf{a}}_{0}
, and
{\mathsf{b}}_{0}

are in disjoint equivalence classes.
Hence,
\mathscr{U}_{\iota\rightarrow\iota}=\{[{\mathsf{f}}_{0}],[{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>{\mathsf{a}}}],[{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>{\mathsf{b}}}],\dots\}
and
\mathscr{U}_{\iota}=\{[{\mathsf{a}}_{0}],[{\mathsf{b}}_{0}]\}
.

When defining the universe
\mathscr{U}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}
of the higher-order interpretation,
we need to ensure that it contains subsets of function spaces,
since
\mathscr{J}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\rightarrow)(\mathscr{D}_{1},\mathscr{D}_{2})
must be a subset of the function space
from
\mathscr{D}_{1}
to
\mathscr{D}_{2}
for all
\mathscr{D}_{1},\mathscr{D}_{2}\in\mathscr{U}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}
.
But the first-order universes
\mathscr{U}_{\tau}
consist of equivalence classes of terms from
\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GF}}

w.r.t.
the rewriting system
R
, not of functions.

To repair this mismatch, we will define a family of functions
\mathscr{E}_{\tau}
that give a meaning
to the elements of the first-order universes
\mathscr{U}_{\tau}
.
We will define a domain
\mathscr{D}_{\tau}
for each ground type
\tau

and then let
\mathscr{U}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}
be the set of all these domains
\mathscr{D}_{\tau}
.
Thus, there will be a one-to-one
correspondence between ground types and domains.
Since the higher-order and first-order type signatures are identical
(including
{\rightarrow}
, which is uninterpreted in first-order logic),
we can identify higher-order and first-order types.

We define
\mathscr{E}_{\tau}
and

\mathscr{D}_{\tau}
in a mutual recursion and prove that
\mathscr{E}_{\tau}
is a bijection
simultaneously. We start with nonfunctional types
\tau
:
Let
\mathscr{D}_{\tau}=\mathscr{U}_{\tau}
and
let
\mathscr{E}_{\tau}:\mathscr{U}_{\tau}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\tau}

be the identity.
We proceed by defining
\mathscr{E}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
and
\mathscr{D}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
.
We assume that
\mathscr{E}_{\tau}
,
\mathscr{E}_{\upsilon}
,

\mathscr{D}_{\tau}
, and
\mathscr{D}_{\upsilon}
have already been defined and that

\mathscr{E}_{\tau}
,
\mathscr{E}_{\upsilon}
are bijections. To ensure that

\mathscr{E}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
will be bijective, we first define an injective
function

\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}:(\mathscr{U}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\tau})\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\upsilon}
,
define
\mathscr{D}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
as its image

\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}(\mathscr{U}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon})
, and finally
define
\mathscr{E}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
as
\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
with its codomain
restricted to
\mathscr{D}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}:\mathscr{U}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\tau}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\upsilon}

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s)\rrbracket_{R}})\bigl(\mathscr{E}_{\tau}\bigl(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(u)\rrbracket_{R}}\bigr)\bigr)=\mathscr{E}_{\upsilon}\bigl(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s\>u)\rrbracket_{R}}\bigr)

This is a valid definition because each element of

\smash{\mathscr{U}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}}
is of the form
\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s)\rrbracket_{R}}
for
some
s
and each element of
\mathscr{D}_{\tau}
is of the form

\mathscr{E}_{\tau}\bigl(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(u)\rrbracket_{R}}\bigr)
for some
u
. This function is
well defined if it does not depend on the choice of
s
and
u
. To show this,
we assume that there are other ground terms
t
and
v
such that

\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s)\rrbracket_{R}}=\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\rrbracket_{R}}
and

\mathscr{E}_{\tau}\bigl(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(u)\rrbracket_{R}}\bigr)=\mathscr{E}_{\tau}\bigl(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(v)\rrbracket_{R}}\bigr)
. Since
\mathscr{E}_{\tau}
is
bijective, we have
\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(u)\rrbracket_{R}}=\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(v)\rrbracket_{R}}
.
Using the
\sim
-notation, we can write this as
u\sim v
.
Applying Lemma
4.7
to the term
x\>y
and the
substitutions
\{x\mapsto s,y\mapsto u\}
and
\{x\mapsto t,y\mapsto v\}
,
we obtain
s\>u\sim t\>v
—i.e.,

\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s\>u)\rrbracket_{R}}=\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t\>v)\rrbracket_{R}}
.
Thus,
\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
is well defined.
It remains to show that
\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
is injective as a function
from
\smash{\mathscr{U}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}}
to

\mathscr{D}_{\tau}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\upsilon}
. Assume two
terms
s,t\in\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GH}}
such that for all
u\in\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GH}}
, we have

\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s\>u)\rrbracket_{R}}=\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t\>u)\rrbracket_{R}}
.
By Lemma
4.6
, it follows that
\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s)\rrbracket_{R}}=\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\rrbracket_{R}}
, which concludes the proof that

\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
is injective.

We define
\mathscr{D}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}=\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}(\mathscr{U}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon})
and

\mathscr{E}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}(a)=\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}(a)
.
This ensures that
\mathscr{E}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}
is bijective and concludes the
inductive definition of
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{E}
. In the following, we will usually
write
\mathscr{E}
instead of
\mathscr{E}_{\tau}
, since the type
\tau
is determined by the first
argument of
\mathscr{E}_{\tau}
.

In our running example, we thus have

\mathscr{D}_{\iota}=\mathscr{U}_{\iota}=\{[{\mathsf{a}}_{0}],[{\mathsf{b}}_{0}]\}

and
\mathscr{E}_{\iota}
is the identity
\mathscr{U}_{\iota}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\iota},\ c\mapsto c
.
The function
\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\iota\rightarrow\iota}
maps
[{\mathsf{f}}_{0}]
to the identity
\mathscr{D}_{\iota}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\iota},\ c\mapsto c
;
it maps
[{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>{\mathsf{a}}}]
to the constant function
\mathscr{D}_{\iota}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\iota},\ c\mapsto[{\mathsf{a}}_{0}]
; and
it maps
[{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>{\mathsf{b}}}]
to the constant function
\mathscr{D}_{\iota}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\iota},\ c\mapsto[{\mathsf{b}}_{0}]
.
The swapping function
[{\mathsf{a}}_{0}]\mapsto[{\mathsf{b}}_{0}],[{\mathsf{b}}_{0}]\mapsto[{\mathsf{a}}_{0}]
is not in the image of
\mathscr{E}^{0}_{\iota\rightarrow\iota}
.
Therefore,
\mathscr{D}_{\iota\rightarrow\iota}
contains only the identity and the two constant functions, but not this swapping function.

We define the higher-order universe as
\mathscr{U}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}=\{\mathscr{D}_{\tau}\mid\tau\text{ ground}\}
.
Moreover, we define

\mathscr{J}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\kappa)(\mathscr{D}_{\bar{\tau}})=\mathscr{U}_{\kappa(\bar{\tau})}

for all
\kappa\in\mathrm{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{ty}}
,
completing the type interpretation
\mathscr{I}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{\mathrm{GH}}=(\mathscr{U}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\mathscr{J}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{{\mathrm{GH}}})
.
We define the interpretation function as

\mathscr{J}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}({\mathsf{f}},\mathscr{D}_{\bar{\upsilon}_{m}})=\mathscr{E}(\mathscr{J}({\mathsf{f}}_{0}^{\bar{\upsilon}_{m}}))

for all
{\mathsf{f}}:\mathsf{\Pi}\bar{\alpha}_{m}.\;\tau
.

In our example, we thus have
\mathscr{J}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}({\mathsf{f}})=\mathscr{E}([{\mathsf{f}}_{0}])
,
which is the identity on
\mathscr{D}_{\iota}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\relbar}\kern-3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}$\blacksquare$\hss}\kern-3.87495pt}\joinrel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{\iota}
.

Finally, we need to define the designation function
\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}
, which takes a
valuation
\xi
and a
\lambda
-expression as arguments. Given a valuation

\xi
, we choose a grounding substitution
\theta
such that

\mathscr{D}_{\alpha\theta}=\xi(\alpha)\text{ and }\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(x\theta)\rrbracket_{R}})=\xi(x)

for all type variables
\alpha
and all variables
x
.
Such a substitution can be constructed as follows:
We can fulfill the first equation in a unique way
because there is a one-to-one correspondence between ground types and domains.
Since
\mathscr{E}^{-1}(\xi(x))
is an element of a first-order universe and
R
is term-generated, there exists
a ground term
t
such that
\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{R}^{\xi}}=\mathscr{E}^{-1}(\xi(x))
.
Choosing one such
t
and defining
x\theta=\mathcalx{F}^{-1}\!(t)
gives us a grounding
substitution
\theta
with the desired property.

We define

\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi,(\lambda x.\>t))=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda x.\>t)\theta)\rrbracket_{R}})
. To prove that
this is well defined, we assume that there exists another substitution
\theta^{\prime}

with the properties
\smash{\mathscr{D}_{\alpha\theta^{\prime}}}=\xi(\alpha)
for all
\alpha

and
\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(x\theta^{\prime})\rrbracket_{R}})=\xi(x)
for
all
x
. Then
we have
\alpha\theta=\alpha\theta^{\prime}
for all
\alpha

due to the one-to-one correspondence between domains and ground types.
We have
\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(x\theta)\rrbracket_{R}}=\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(x\theta^{\prime})\rrbracket_{R}}
for all
x
because
\mathscr{E}
is
injective.
By Lemma
4.7
it follows that

\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda x.\>t)\theta)\rrbracket_{R}}=\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda x.\>t)\theta^{\prime})\rrbracket_{R}}
, which proves that
\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}
is
well defined.

In our example, for all
\xi
we have
\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi,\lambda x.\>x)=\mathscr{E}([{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>x}])=\mathscr{E}([{\mathsf{f}}_{0}])
, which is the identity.
If
\xi(y)=[{\mathsf{a}}_{0}]
, then
\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi,\lambda x.\>y)=\mathscr{E}([{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>{\mathsf{a}}}])
, which is the constant function
c\mapsto[{\mathsf{a}}_{0}]
.
Similarly, if
\xi(y)=[{\mathsf{b}}_{0}]
, then
\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi,\lambda x.\>y)
is the constant function
c\mapsto[{\mathsf{b}}_{0}]
.

This concludes the definition of the interpretation

\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}=(\mathscr{U}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\mathscr{J}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\mathscr{J}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}})
. It remains to show that
\smash{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}

is proper. In a proper interpretation, the denotation
\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}}
of a
term
t
does not depend on the representative of
t
modulo
\beta\eta
, but
since we have not yet shown
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}
to be proper, we cannot rely on
this property. For this reason, we use
\lambda
-terms in the following three
lemmas and mark all
\beta\eta
-reductions explicitly.

The higher-order interpretation
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}
relates to the first-order
interpretation
R
as follows:

Lemma 4.8

Given a ground
\lambda
-term
t
, we have

\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}}=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})

Proof

By induction on
t
.
Assume that
\smash{\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}}=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})
for all proper subterms
s
of
t
.
If
t
is of the form
{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}
, then

\displaystyle\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{J}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}({\mathsf{f}},\mathscr{D}_{\bar{\tau}})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\mathscr{J}({\mathsf{f}}_{0},\mathscr{U}_{\mathcalx{F}\!(\bar{\tau})}))

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket{\mathsf{f}}_{0}{\langle\mathcalx{F}\!(\bar{\tau})\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\rrbracket_{R}})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!({{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}})\rrbracket_{R}})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!({\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})

If
t
is an application
t=t_{1}\>t_{2}
, where
t_{1}
is of type
\tau\rightarrow\upsilon
, then

\displaystyle\smash{\llbracket t_{1}\>t_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}}

\displaystyle=\smash{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}}(\smash{\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}})

\displaystyle\overset{\!\scriptscriptstyle\text{IH}\!}{=}\mathscr{E}_{\tau\rightarrow\upsilon}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t_{1}\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})(\mathscr{E}_{\tau}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t_{2}\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}}))

\displaystyle\overset{\kern-28.45274pt\text{Def }\mathscr{E}\kern-28.45274pt}{=}\enskip\mathscr{E}_{\upsilon}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((t_{1}\>t_{2})\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})

If
t
is a
\lambda
-expression, then

\displaystyle\smash{\llbracket\lambda x.\>u\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi,(\lambda x.\>u))

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda x.\>u)\theta\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda x.\>u)\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})

where
\theta

is a substitution such that
\mathscr{D}_{\alpha\theta}=\xi(\alpha)
and

\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(x\theta)\rrbracket_{R}})=\xi(x)
.
∎

We need to show that the interpretation
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}=(\mathscr{U}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\mathscr{J}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\mathscr{J}^{{\mathrm{GH}}},\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}})
is
proper. In the proof, we will need to employ the following lemma, which is
very similar to the substitution lemma (Lemma
3.17
),
but we
must prove it here for our particular interpretation
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}
because we have not
shown that
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}
is proper yet.

Lemma 4.9
(Substitution lemma)

\smash{\llbracket\tau\rho\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{\mathrm{GH}}}^{\xi}}=\smash{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{\mathrm{GH}}}^{\xi^{\prime}}}

and

\vphantom{(_{(_{(}}}\smash{\llbracket t\rho\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi}}=\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi^{\prime}}}

for all
\lambda
-terms
t
,
all
\tau\in\mathcalx{Ty}_{\mathrm{H}}
and all grounding substitutions
\rho
,
where
\xi^{\prime}(\alpha)=\vphantom{(_{(_{(}}}\smash{\llbracket\alpha\rho\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathsf{ty}}^{\mathrm{GH}}}^{\xi}}
for all type variables
\alpha

and
\xi^{\prime}(x)=\smash{\llbracket x\rho\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi}}
for all term variables
x
.

Proof

We proceed by induction on the structure of
\tau
and
t
.
The proof is identical to the one of Lemma
3.17
,
except for the last step, which uses properness of the interpretation, a
property we cannot assume here.
However, here, we have the assumption that
\rho
is a grounding substitution.
Therefore, if
t
is a
\lambda
-expression, we argue as follows:

\displaystyle\smash{\llbracket(\lambda z.\>u)\rho\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\smash{\llbracket(\lambda z.\>u\rho^{\prime})\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi}}

where
\rho^{\prime}(z)=z
and
\rho^{\prime}(x)=\rho(x)
for
x\neq z

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi,(\lambda z.\>u\rho^{\prime}))

by the definition of the term denotation

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda z.\>u)\rho\theta\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}^{\xi}})

for some
\theta
by the definition of
\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda z.\>u)\rho\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}^{\xi}})

because
(\lambda z.\>u)\rho
is ground

\displaystyle\overset{\smash{*}}{=}\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi^{\prime},\lambda z.\>u)

by the definition of
\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}
and Lemma
4.8

\displaystyle=\smash{\llbracket\lambda z.\>u\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi^{\prime}}}

by the definition of the term denotation

The step
*
is justified as follows:
We have

\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi^{\prime},\lambda z.\>u)=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda z.\>u)\theta^{\prime}\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}^{\xi}})

by the definition of
\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}
,
if
\theta^{\prime}
is a substitution such that

\smash{\mathscr{D}_{\alpha\theta^{\prime}}}=\xi^{\prime}(\alpha)
for all
\alpha
and

\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(x\theta^{\prime}\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}^{\xi}})=\xi^{\prime}(x)

for all
x
.
By the definition of
\xi^{\prime}
and by Lemma
4.8
,

\rho
is such a substitution.
Hence,

\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi^{\prime},\lambda z.\>u)=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda z.\>u)\rho\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}^{\xi}})
.
∎

Lemma 4.10

The interpretation
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}
is proper.

Proof

We must show that

\smash{\llbracket(\lambda x.\>t)\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi}}(a)=\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi[x\mapsto a]}}

for all
\lambda
-expressions
\lambda x.\>t
, all valuations
\xi
, and all values
a
.

\displaystyle\smash{\llbracket\lambda x.\>t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi}}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}(\xi,\lambda x.\>t)(a)

by the definition of
\smash{\llbracket\phantom{\cdot}\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!((\lambda x.\>t)\theta\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})(a)

by the definition of
\mathscr{L}^{{\mathrm{GH}}}
for some
\theta

such that
\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(z\theta)\rrbracket_{R}})=\xi(z)
for all
z

and
\smash{\mathscr{D}}_{\alpha\theta}=\xi(\alpha)
for all
\alpha

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(((\lambda x.\>t)\theta\;s)\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})

by the definition of
\mathscr{E}

where
\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(s)\rrbracket_{R}})=a

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t(\theta[x\mapsto s])\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\rrbracket_{R}})

by
\beta
-reduction

\displaystyle=\smash{\llbracket t(\theta[x\mapsto s])\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}}

by Lemma
4.8

\displaystyle=\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}^{\xi[x\mapsto a]}}

by Lemma
4.9

∎

Lemma 4.11

\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}
is a model of
N
.

Proof

By Lemma
4.8
,
we have

\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}}}=\mathscr{E}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\rrbracket_{R}})

for all
t\in\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GH}}
.
Since
\mathscr{E}
is a bijection,
it follows that any (dis)equation
s\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}t\in\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
is true in
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}

if and only if
\mathcalx{F}\!(s\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}t)
is true in
R
.
Hence, a clause
C\in\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
is true in
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}

if and only if
\mathcalx{F}\!(C)
is true in
R
.
By
Theorem
4.5

and the assumption
that
\bot\notin N
,
R
is a model of
\mathcalx{F}\!(N)
—that is, for all clauses
C\in N
,
\mathcalx{F}\!(C)
is
true in
R
.
Hence, all clauses
C\in N
are true in
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}
and therefore

\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}
is a model of
N
.
∎

We summarize the results of this subsection in the following theorem:

Theorem 4.12
(Ground static refutational completeness)

Let
\mathit{GHSel}
be a selection function on
\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
.
Then the inference system
\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
is statically refutationally complete

w.r.t.

(\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}},\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}})
.
In other words, if
N\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
is a clause set saturated
w.r.t.

\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
and
\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
,
then
N\models\bot
if and only if
\bot\in N
.

The construction of
\mathscr{I}^{\smash{{\mathrm{GH}}}}
relies on specific properties of
R
. It
would not work with an arbitrary first-order interpretation. Transforming a
higher-order interpretation into a first-order interpretation is easier:

Lemma 4.13

Given a clausal higher-order interpretation
\mathscr{I}

on
{\mathrm{GH}}
,
there exists a first-order interpretation
\mathscr{I}^{\mathrm{GF}}
on
{\mathrm{GF}}

such that for any clause
C\in\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
the truth values of

C
in
\mathscr{I}
and of
\mathcalx{F}\!(C)
in
\mathscr{I}^{\mathrm{GF}}
coincide.

Proof

Let
\mathscr{I}=(\mathscr{I}_{\mathsf{ty}},\mathscr{J},\mathscr{L})
be a clausal higher-order interpretation.
Let
\mathscr{U}^{\mathrm{GF}}_{\tau}=\smash{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathsf{ty}}}}
be the first-order type universe for the ground type
\tau
.
For a symbol
\smash{{\mathsf{f}}^{\bar{\upsilon}}_{\!j}}\in\mathrm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{GF}}
, let
\mathscr{J}^{\mathrm{GF}}(\smash{{\mathsf{f}}^{\bar{\upsilon}}_{\!j}})=\smash{\llbracket{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}}}
(up to currying).
For a symbol
{\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>t}\in\mathrm{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{GF}}
,
let
\mathscr{J}^{\mathrm{GF}}({\mathsf{lam}}_{\lambda x.\>t})=\smash{\llbracket\lambda x.\>t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}}}
.
This defines a first-order interpretation
\mathscr{I}^{\mathrm{GF}}=(\mathscr{U}^{\mathrm{GF}},\mathscr{J}^{\mathrm{GF}})
.

We need to show that for any
C\in\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
,
\mathscr{I}\models C
if and only if
\mathscr{I}^{\mathrm{GF}}\models\mathcalx{F}\!(C)
.
It suffices to show that
\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}}}=\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\mathrm{GF}}}}

for all terms
t\in\mathcalx{T}_{\mathrm{GH}}
.
We prove this by induction on the structure of the
\eta
-short
\beta
-normal form of
t
.
If
t
is a
\lambda
-expression, this is obvious.
If
t
is of the form
{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>\bar{s}_{\!j}
, then

\mathcalx{F}\!(t)=\smash{{\mathsf{f}}^{\bar{\upsilon}}_{\!j}}(\mathcalx{F}\!(\bar{s}_{\!j}))

and hence

\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(t)\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\mathrm{GF}}}}=\mathscr{J}^{\mathrm{GF}}(\smash{{\mathsf{f}}^{\bar{\upsilon}}_{\!j}})(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(\bar{s}_{\!j})\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\mathrm{GF}}}})=\smash{\llbracket{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}}}(\smash{\llbracket\mathcalx{F}\!(\bar{s}_{\!j})\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}^{\mathrm{GF}}}})\overset{\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\text{IH}}}{=}\smash{\llbracket{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}}}(\smash{\llbracket\bar{s}_{\!j}\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}}})=\smash{\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathscr{I}}}
.
∎

4.4

The Nonground Higher-Order Level

To lift the result to the nonground level, we employ the saturation framework of
Waldmann et al.
waldmann-et-al-2020-saturation
.
It is easy to see that the entailment relation
\models
on
{\mathrm{GH}}
is a consequence relation in the sense of the framework.
We need to show that our redundancy criterion on
{\mathrm{GH}}
is a redundancy criterion in the sense of the framework and that

{\mathcalx{G}}
is a grounding function in the sense of the framework:

Lemma 4.14

The redundancy criterion for
{\mathrm{GH}}
is a redundancy criterion
in the sense of Sect. 2 of the saturation framework.

Proof

We must prove the conditions (R1) to (R4) of the saturation framework.
Adapted to our context, they state the following
for all clause sets
N,N^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
:

(R1)

if
N\models\bot
, then
N\setminus\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}}(N)\models\bot
;

(R2)

if
N\subseteq N^{\prime}
, then
\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}}(N)\subseteq\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}}(N^{\prime})

and
\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}(N)\subseteq\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}(N^{\prime})
;

(R3)

if
N^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}}(N)
, then
\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}}(N)\subseteq\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}}(N\setminus N^{\prime})

and
\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}(N)\subseteq\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}(N\setminus N^{\prime})
;

(R4)

if
\iota\in\mathit{GHInf}
and
\mathit{concl}(\iota)\in N
, then
\iota\in\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}(N)
.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma 4.10 of Bentkamp et al.
bentkamp-et-al-lfhosup-arxiv
,
using Lemma
4.13
. ∎

Lemma 4.15

The grounding functions
{\mathcalx{G}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
for
\mathit{GHSel}\in{\mathcalx{G}}(\mathit{HSel})

are grounding functions in the sense of Sect. 3 of the saturation framework.

Proof

We must prove the conditions (G1), (G2), and (G3) of the saturation framework.
Adapted to our context, they state the following:

(G1)

{\mathcalx{G}}(\bot)=\{\bot\}
;

(G2)

for every
C\in\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{H}}
, if
\bot\in{\mathcalx{G}}(C)
, then
C=\bot
;

(G3)

for every
\iota\in\mathit{HInf}
,
{\mathcalx{G}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}(\iota)\subseteq\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}({\mathcalx{G}}(\mathit{concl}(\iota)))
.

Clearly,
C=\bot
if and only if
\bot\in{\mathcalx{G}}(C)
if and only if
{\mathcalx{G}}(C)=\{\bot\}
, proving (G1) and (G2).
For every
\iota\in\mathit{HInf}
, by the definition of
{\mathcalx{G}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
,
we have
\mathit{concl}({\mathcalx{G}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}(\iota))\subseteq{\mathcalx{G}}(\mathit{concl}(\iota))
, and thus (G3) by (R4). ∎

To lift the completeness result of the previous subsection to
the nonground calculus
\mathit{HInf}
,
we employ Theorem 14 of the saturation framework, which,
adapted to
our context, is stated as follows.
The theorem uses the notation
\mathit{Inf}(N)

to denote the set of
\mathit{Inf}
-inferences
whose premises are in
N
,
for an inference system
\mathit{Inf}
and a clause set
N
.
Moreover, it uses
Herbrand entailment
\models_{\mathcalx{G}}
on
\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{H}}
, which is
defined so that
N_{1}\models_{\mathcalx{G}}N_{2}
if and only if
{\mathcalx{G}}(N_{1})\models{\mathcalx{G}}(N_{2})
.

Theorem 4.16
(Lifting theorem)

If
\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
is statically refutationally complete

w.r.t.

(\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathit{GHSel}},\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}})

for every
\mathit{GHSel}\in{\mathcalx{G}}(\mathit{HSel})
,
and if for every
N\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{H}}
that is
saturated
w.r.t.

\mathit{HInf}
and
\mathit{HRed}_{\mathrm{I}}

there exists a
\mathit{GHSel}\in{\mathcalx{G}}(\mathit{HSel})

such that

\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}({\mathcalx{G}}(N))\subseteq{\mathcalx{G}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}(\mathit{HInf}(N))\cup\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}({\mathcalx{G}}(N))
,
then also

\mathit{HInf}
is statically refutationally complete
w.r.t.

(\mathit{HRed}_{\mathrm{I}},{\mathit{HRed}}_{\mathrm{C}})

and
\models_{\mathcalx{G}}
.

Proof

This is almost an instance of Theorem 14 of the saturation framework.
We take
\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{H}}
for
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{GH}}
for
\mathbf{G}
, and
{\mathcalx{G}}(\mathit{HSel})
for
Q
.
It is easy to see that the entailment relation
\models
on
{\mathrm{GH}}
is a consequence relation in the sense
of the framework. By Lemma
4.14
and
4.15
,

(\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathit{GHSel}},\allowbreak\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{C}})
is a redundancy criterion
in the sense of the framework,
and
{\mathcalx{G}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
are grounding functions
in the sense of the framework,
for all
\mathit{GHSel}\in{\mathcalx{G}}(\mathit{HSel})
.
The redundancy criterion
(\mathit{HRed}_{\mathrm{I}},{\mathit{HRed}}_{\mathrm{C}})

matches exactly the intersected lifted redundancy criterion

\mathit{Red}^{\mathrel{\cap},\sqsupset}

of the saturation framework.
Their Theorem 14 states the theorem only for
{\sqsupset}=\varnothing
.
By their Lemma 16, it also holds if
{\sqsupset}\not=\varnothing
. ∎

Let
N\subseteq\mathcalx{C}_{\mathrm{H}}
be a clause set saturated
w.r.t.

\mathit{HInf}
and
\mathit{HRed}_{\mathrm{I}}
.
We assume that
\mathit{HSel}
fulfills the selection restriction that
a literal
L\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,\,y\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}
must not be selected if
y\>\bar{u}_{n}
, with
n>0
, is a
\succeq
-maximal term of the clause,
as required in Definition
3.8
.
For the above theorem to apply,
we need to show that there exists a selection
function
\mathit{GHSel}\in{\mathcalx{G}}(\mathit{HSel})
such that
all inferences
\iota\in\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
with
\mathit{prems}(\iota)\in{\mathcalx{G}}(N)

are liftable or redundant.
Here, for
\iota
to be
liftable
means that
\iota
is a
\smash{{\mathcalx{G}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}}
-ground instance
of a
\smash{\mathit{HInf}}
-inference from
N
;
for
\iota
to be
redundant
means that
\iota\in\smash{\mathit{GHRed}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathit{GHSel}}({\mathcalx{G}}(N))}
.

To choose the right selection function
\mathit{GHSel}\in{\mathcalx{G}}(\mathit{HSel})
, we observe that each ground clause

C\in{\mathcalx{G}}(N)
must have at least one corresponding clause
D\in N
such that
C
is a ground instance of
D
.
We choose one of them for each
C\in{\mathcalx{G}}(N)
, which we denote by
{\mathcalx{G}}^{-1}(C)
. Then let
\mathit{GHSel}
select those literals in
C
that
correspond to literals selected by
\mathit{HSel}
in
{\mathcalx{G}}^{-1}(C)
. With respect to this selection function

\mathit{GHSel}
, we can show that all inferences from
{\mathcalx{G}}(N)
are liftable or redundant:

Lemma 4.17

Let
{\mathcalx{G}}^{-1}(C)=D\in N
and
D\theta=C
.
Let
\sigma
and
\rho
be substitutions such that

x\sigma\rho=x\theta
for all variables
x
in
D
.
Let
L
be a (strictly)
\succeq
-eligible literal in
C

w.r.t.

\mathit{GHSel}
.
Then there exists a (strictly)
\succsim
-eligible literal
L^{\prime}
in
D

w.r.t.

\sigma
and
\mathit{HSel}
such that
L^{\prime}\theta=L
.

Proof

If
L\in\mathit{GHSel}(C)
, then there exists
L^{\prime}
such that
L^{\prime}\theta=L
and
L^{\prime}\in\mathit{HSel}(D)
by the definition of
{\mathcalx{G}}^{-1}
.
Otherwise,
L
is
\succeq
-maximal in
C
.
Since
C=D\sigma\rho
, there are literals
L^{\prime}
in
D\sigma
such that
L^{\prime}\rho=L
.
Choose
L^{\prime}
to be a
\succsim
-maximal among them.
Then
L^{\prime}
is
\succsim
-maximal in
D\sigma
because for any literal
L^{\prime\prime}\in D
with
L^{\prime\prime}\succsim L^{\prime}
,
we have
L^{\prime\prime}\rho\succeq L^{\prime}\rho=L
and hence
L^{\prime\prime}\rho=L
by
\succeq
-maximality of
L
.

If
L
is strictly
\succeq
-maximal in
C
,

L^{\prime}
is also strictly
\succsim
-maximal in
D\sigma
because a duplicate of
L^{\prime}
in
D\sigma
would imply a duplicate of
L
in
C
.
∎

Lemma 4.18
(Lifting of
ERes
,
EFact
,
GArgCong
, and
GExt
)

All
ERes
,
EFact
,
GArgCong
, and
GExt
inferences from
{\mathcalx{G}}(N)
are liftable.

Proof

ERes
: Let
\iota\in\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
be an
ERes
inference with
\mathit{prems}(\iota)\in{\mathcalx{G}}(N)
.
Then
\iota
is of the form

\displaystyle{C\theta~=~C^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}s\theta\not\approx s^{\prime}\theta}\mathstrut

ERes

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\theta\mathstrut

where
{\mathcalx{G}}^{-1}(C\theta)=C=C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\not\approx s^{\prime}

and the literal
s\theta\not\approx s^{\prime}\theta

is
\succeq
-eligible
w.r.t.

\mathit{GHSel}
.
Since
s\theta
and
s^{\prime}\theta
are unifiable and ground,
we have
s\theta=s^{\prime}\theta
.
Thus, there exists an idempotent
\sigma\in\csu(s,s^{\prime})

such that for some substitution
\rho
and for all variables
x
in
C
,
we have
x\sigma\rho=x\theta
.
By Lemma
4.17
, we may assume without loss of generality that

s\not\approx s^{\prime}
is
\succsim
-eligible in
C

w.r.t.

\sigma
and
\mathit{HSel}
.
Hence, the following inference
\iota^{\prime}\in\mathit{HInf}
is applicable:

\displaystyle{C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\not\approx s^{\prime}}\mathstrut

ERes

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\sigma\mathstrut

Then
\iota
is the
\sigma\rho
-ground instance of
\iota^{\prime}

and is therefore liftable.

EFact
: Analogously, if
\iota\in\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}

is an
EFact
inference with
\mathit{prems}(\iota)\in{\mathcalx{G}}(N)
,
then
\iota
is of the form

\displaystyle{C\theta~=~C^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}s^{\prime}\theta\approx t^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}s\theta\approx t\theta}\mathstrut

EFact

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}t\theta\not\approx t^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}s\theta\approx t^{\prime}\theta\mathstrut

where
{\mathcalx{G}}^{-1}(C\theta)=C=C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s^{\prime}\approx t^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\approx t
,
the literal
s\theta\approx t\theta
is
\succeq
-eligible in
C

w.r.t.

\mathit{GHSel}
,
and
s\theta\not\prec t\theta
. Then
s\not\prec t
.
Moreover,
s\theta
and
s^{\prime}\theta
are unifiable and ground.
Hence,

s\theta=s^{\prime}\theta
and
there exists an idempotent
\sigma\in\csu(s,s^{\prime})

such that for some substitution
\rho
and for all variables
x
in
C
,
we have
x\sigma\rho=x\theta
.
By Lemma
4.17
, we may assume without loss of generality that

s\approx t
is
\succsim
-eligible in
C

w.r.t.

\sigma
and
\mathit{HSel}
.
It follows that the following inference
\iota^{\prime}\in\mathit{HInf}
is applicable:

\displaystyle{C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s^{\prime}\approx t^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\approx t}\mathstrut

EFact

\displaystyle(C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}t\not\approx t^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\approx t^{\prime})\sigma\mathstrut

Then
\iota
is the
\sigma\rho
-ground instance of
\iota^{\prime}

and is therefore liftable.

GArgCong
:
Let
\iota\in\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
be a
GArgCong
inference with
\mathit{prems}(\iota)\in{\mathcalx{G}}(N)
.
Then
\iota
is of the form

\displaystyle{C\theta~=~C^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}s\theta\approx s^{\prime}\theta}\mathstrut

GArgCong

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}s\theta\>\bar{u}_{n}\approx s^{\prime}\theta\>\bar{u}_{n}\mathstrut

where
{\mathcalx{G}}^{-1}(C\theta)=C=C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\approx s^{\prime}
,
the literal
s\theta\approx s^{\prime}\theta

is strictly
\succeq
-eligible
w.r.t.

\mathit{GHSel}
, and

s\theta
and
s^{\prime}\theta
are of functional type.
It follows that
s
and
s^{\prime}
have either a functional
or a polymorphic type.
Let
\sigma
be the most general substitution such that

s\sigma
and
s^{\prime}\sigma
take
n
arguments.
By Lemma
4.17
, we may assume without loss of generality that

s\not\approx s^{\prime}
is strictly
\succsim
-eligible in
C

w.r.t.

\sigma
and
\mathit{HSel}
.
Hence the following inference
\iota^{\prime}\in\mathit{HInf}
is applicable:

\displaystyle{C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\approx s^{\prime}}\mathstrut

ArgCong

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\sigma\mathrel{\lor}s\sigma\>\bar{x}_{n}\approx s^{\prime}\sigma\>\bar{x}_{n}\mathstrut

Since
\sigma
is the most general substitution
that ensures well-typedness of the conclusion,

\iota
is a ground instance of
\iota^{\prime}

and is therefore liftable.

GExt
: The conclusion of a
GExt
inference in
\mathit{GHInf}
is by definition a
ground instance of the conclusion of an
Ext
inference in
\mathit{HInf}
.
Hence, the
GExt
inference is a ground instance of the
Ext
inference.
Therefore it is liftable.
∎

Some of the
Sup
inferences in
\mathit{GHInf}
are liftable as well:

Lemma 4.19
(Instances of green subterms)

Let
s
be a
\lambda
-term in
\eta
-short
\beta
-normal form,
let
\sigma
be a substitution, and
let
p
be a green position of both
s
and
s\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}
.
Then
(s|_{p})\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}=(s\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})|_{p}
.

Proof

By induction on
p
.
If
p=\varepsilon
, then
(s|_{p})\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}=s\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}=(s\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})|_{p}
.
If
p=i.p^{\prime}
, then

s={\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>s_{1}\dots s_{n}
and

s\sigma={\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\sigma\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>(s_{1}\sigma)\dots(s_{n}\sigma)
, where
1\leq i\leq n
and

p^{\prime}
is a green position of
s_{i}
.
Clearly,

\beta\eta
-normalization steps of
s\sigma
can take place only in proper subterms.
So

s\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}={\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\sigma\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>(s_{1}\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})\dots(s_{n}\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}).
Since
p=i.p^{\prime}
is a green position of
s\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}
,

p^{\prime}
must be a green position of
(s_{i}\sigma)\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}
.
By the induction hypothesis,

(s_{i}|_{p^{\prime}})\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}=(s_{i}\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})|_{p^{\prime}}
.
Therefore

(s|_{p})\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}=(s|_{i.p^{\prime}})\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}=(s_{i}|_{p^{\prime}})\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta}=(s_{i}\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})|_{p^{\prime}}=(s\sigma\kern 0.83328pt{\downarrow}_{\beta\eta})|_{p}
.
∎

Lemma 4.20
(Lifting of
Sup
)

Let
\iota\in\mathit{GHInf}^{\mathit{GHSel}}
be a
Sup
inference

\displaystyle{\overbrace{D^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}t\theta\approx t^{\prime}\theta}^{\vphantom{\cdot}\smash{D\theta}}\hskip 12.50002pt\overbrace{C^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}s\theta\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,t\theta\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}_{p}\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}s^{\prime}\theta}^{\vphantom{\cdot}\smash{C\theta}}}\mathstrut

Sup

\displaystyle D^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}C^{\prime}\theta\mathrel{\lor}s\theta\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,t^{\prime}\theta\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}_{p}\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}s^{\prime}\theta\mathstrut

where
{\mathcalx{G}}^{-1}(D\theta)=D=D^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}t\approx t^{\prime}\in N
,
s\theta=s\theta\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,t\theta\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}_{p}
, and

{\mathcalx{G}}^{-1}(C\theta)=C=C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}s^{\prime}\in N
.
We assume that
s
,
t
,
s\theta
, and
t\theta
are represented by
\lambda
-terms
in
\eta
-short
\beta
-normal form.
Let
p^{\prime}
be the longest prefix of
p

that is a green position of
s
.
Since
\varepsilon
is a green position of
s
,
the longest prefix always exists.
Let
u=s|_{p^{\prime}}
.
Suppose one of the following conditions applies:
(i)

u
is a deeply occurring variable in
C
;

(ii)

p=p^{\prime}
and the variable condition holds for
D
and
C
; or

(iii)

p\neq p^{\prime}
and
u
is not a variable.
Then
\iota
is liftable.

Proof

The
Sup
inference conditions for
\iota
are that

t\theta\approx t^{\prime}\theta
is strictly
\succeq
-eligible,

s\theta\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}s^{\prime}\theta
is strictly
\succeq
-eligible if positive
and
\succeq
-eligible if negative,

D\theta\not\succsim C\theta
,

t\theta\not\precsim t^{\prime}\theta
, and
s\theta\not\precsim s^{\prime}\theta
.
We assume that

s
,
t
,
s\theta
, and
t\theta
are represented by
\lambda
-terms
in
\eta
-short
\beta
-normal form.
By Lemma
4.19
,

u\theta
agrees with
s\theta|_{p^{\prime}}

(considering both as terms rather than as
\lambda
-terms).

Case 1:
We have (a)
p=p^{\prime}
, (b)
u
is not fluid, and (c)
u
is not a variable deeply occurring in
C
.
Then
u\theta=s\theta|_{p^{\prime}}=s\theta|_{p}=t\theta
.
Since
\theta
is a unifier of
u
and
t
,
there exists an idempotent
\sigma\in\csu(t,u)

such that for some substitution
\rho
and for all variables
x
occurring in
D
and
C
,
we have
x\sigma\rho=x\theta
.
The inference conditions can be lifted:
(Strict) eligibility of
t\theta\approx t^{\prime}\theta
and
s\theta\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}s^{\prime}\theta

w.r.t.

\mathit{GHSel}

implies (strict) eligibility of
t\approx t^{\prime}
and
s\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}s^{\prime}

w.r.t.

\sigma
and
\mathit{HSel}
;

D\theta\not\succsim C\theta
implies
D\not\succsim C
;

t\theta\not\precsim t^{\prime}\theta
implies
t\not\precsim t^{\prime}
; and

s\theta\not\precsim s^{\prime}\theta
implies
s\not\precsim s^{\prime}
.
Moreover, by (a) and (c), condition (ii) must hold and thus the variable condition holds for
D
and
C
.
Hence there is the following
Sup
inference
\iota^{\prime}\in\mathit{HInf}
:

\displaystyle{D^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}t\approx t^{\prime}\hskip 12.50002ptC^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,u\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}_{p}\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}s^{\prime}}\mathstrut

Sup

\displaystyle(D^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}C^{\prime}\mathrel{\lor}s\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,t^{\prime}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}_{p}\mathrel{\dot{\approx}}s^{\prime})\sigma\mathstrut

Then
\iota
is the
\sigma\rho
-ground instance
of
\iota^{\prime}
and therefore liftable.

Case 2:
We have (a)
p\neq p^{\prime}
, or (b)
u
is fluid, or (c)
u
is a variable deeply occurring in
C
.
We will first show that (a) implies (b) or (c).
Suppose (a) holds but neither (b) nor (c) holds.
Then condition (iii) must hold—i.e.,
u
is not a variable.
Moreover, since (b) does not hold,
u
cannot have the form
y\>\bar{u}_{n}
for a variable
y
and
n\geq 1
.
If
u
were of the form

{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>s_{1}\dots{s_{n}}
with
n\geq 0
,

u\theta
would have the form

{\mathsf{f}}{\langle\bar{\tau}\theta\rangle\kern-0.83328pt}\>(s_{1}\theta)\dots(s_{n}\theta)
,
but then there is some
1\leq i\leq n

such that
p^{\prime}.i
is a prefix of
p

and
s|_{p^{\prime}.i}
is a green subterm of
s
,
contradicting the maximality of
p^{\prime}
.
So
u
must be a
\lambda
-expression,
but since
t\theta
is a proper green subterm of
u\theta
,

u\theta
cannot be a
\lambda
-expression,
yielding a contradiction. We may thus assume that (b) or (c) holds.

Let
p=p^{\prime}.p^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
z
be a fresh variable.
Define a substitution
\theta^{\prime}
that maps this variable
z
to
